the Z plate / ball nut standoff, measured 2026-10-08

SPACER_TOP_Z46.6 mmmeasured
used by NUT_STANDOFF_STEP
46.6, the spacers' top face above the X carriage plate - his, 2026-10-08. This is the plane the Z plate lands on, set by the rails and the bearing blocks, which are the precision reference in this stack.
NUT_TOP_Z45.3 mmmeasured
used by NUT_STANDOFF_STEP
45.3, the Z ball nut's top face above the X carriage plate - his, 2026-10-08. It sits BELOW the spacers, so the Z plate laid on the spacers stands off the nut rather than touching it. SETTLED: IT CROSS-CHECKS THE TWO CONSTANTS THAT CAUSED ALL THE TROUBLE, from a third direction. The Z axis has no interposer, so geometry puts this face at SCREW_AXIS_H + NUT_AXIS_TO_FACE = 25 + 20 = 45, and the caliper says 45.3. His 2026-10-10 statement of 45 for Z and 55 for the beams - the same stack plus the interposer - is what made the comparison. Two constants arrived at off a BF12 and off a nut page, checked against a measurement on a different axis, landing within three tenths. OPEN: DO NOT ROUND THIS TO 45: it is measured, and NUT_STANDOFF_STEP subtracts it from a measured SPACER_TOP_Z to get 1.3, which is a tenths-level chain feeding the shim. The 0.3 earns its place.
NUT_STANDOFF_STEP1.3 mmderived
built from SPACER_TOP_Z - NUT_TOP_Z
used by NUT_SHIM
1.3, the gap between the Z plate's back face and the ball nut's mounting face once the plate is down on the spacers. FLAG: IT MUST BE CLOSED WITH METAL, NOT BY TIGHTENING. The nut is captive on the screw, so pulling those four M5 across an open gap does not draw the plate down - it drags the NUT off the screw axis and holds it there for the whole travel, which is a constant side load. Even 0.3 across the nut's 40 mm bolt span is 0.43 degrees of tilt on a part that wants coplanarity in hundredths. Closed as SPACER_SKIM plus NUT_SHIM. OPEN: It is a difference of two readings against a common reference, so the errors add - call it two tenths, which is already too much to settle with an irreversible cut.
NUT_SHIM0.3 mmderived
built from NUT_STANDOFF_STEP - SPACER_SKIM
used by Z_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE
0.3 NOMINAL, and the real one is measured on the assembled stack - feeler-gauge the gap at the nut with the plate already tight on the spacers and blocks, then shim to what you read. Solid shim, steel or brass, which comes in 0.05 steps and so resolves the joint far finer than the step could be measured beforehand. FLAG: THE FOUR M5 CLEARANCE HOLES ALREADY GIVE THE NUT THE FLOAT IT NEEDS - 0.5 radial at Z_HOLE_D - so it self-centres on the screw as it is tightened. The only thing missing was the standoff, and that is all this shim sets: free where it needs to float, rigid where it drives. OPEN: The shim adds to the grip length of those four bolts, so it comes off their engagement into the nut.

the Z carriage bolt stack, settled 2026-10-08

BLOCK_HOLE_DEPTH8.7 mmmeasured
8.7, the drilled depth of the bearing block's M5 holes - his, 2026-10-08, against 8 of thread. FLAG: 0.7 OF CHIP SPACE, SO THERE IS NOTHING TO TAP INTO. The block's 8 really is a hard ceiling, unlike the nut housing's - and it does not matter, because the block is STEEL and 5.8 mm is 1.16 x D there. Two joints that read identically on paper - M5 into an 8 mm thread - and completely different problems, because of what they are cut in.
BLOCK_TAP_DEPTH6 mmvendor
6 - the M5 THREAD depth in an HGH20 block, from the Vevor block page's Mxl column, which reads M5x6. FLAG: IT WAS CARRIED AS 8 AND THAT WAS THE WRONG COLUMN. The 8 is T, the hole depth, and a measured BLOCK_HOLE_DEPTH of 8.7 agreed with it - which is exactly why the error survived: the number checked out against the part, just not against the right feature. OPEN: IT TIGHTENS THE Z BOLT WINDOW HARD. Z_BOLT_ENGAGE is 5.8 against a 6 mm cap, so 0.2 of margin, and the faced spacer must land between 15.0 and 16.0 - thinner and the bolt jams where the thread stops, reading tight while the joint stays loose; thicker and engagement drops under 1 x D. If the faced pair falls outside that, MOVE Z_CBORE_WEB, which is what it is there for. OPEN: The nut housing is a different part with a different answer - see NUT_TAP_DEPTH.
M5_D5 mmnominal
used by SOLE_ROOT_WALL
M5 shank, for the clearances that are measured to a bolt's SIDE rather than to a hole. SOLE_ROOT_WALL is the one that needs it: a hole bored toward a bolt has to stop short of the bolt itself, not of its centreline.
M5_HEAD_D8.48 mmmeasured
used by CAST_HEAD_X_IN
8.48, the socket head cap diameter on the M5 he is actually using - his calipers 2026-10-08, not the 8.5 nominal. An 8.9 counterbore leaves 0.21 radial clearance on it.
M5_HEAD_H5 mmmeasured
used by CAST_HEAD_Z_UP
5, head height on the same bolts. The counterbore has to beat this or the head stands proud.
Z_CBORE_D8.9 mmpart
8.9, the counterbore bit's bore - a piloted 5.2 x 8.9. The pilot size is why the drilling order is forced; see Z_PILOT_D.
Z_PILOT_D5.2 mmpart
5.2, the counterbore's pilot, and therefore the size the hole must be WHILE IT IS COUNTERBORED. FLAG: THE ORDER IS FORCED AND IT IS A ONE-WAY DOOR: drill 5.2, counterbore, THEN open to Z_HOLE_D. Open it first and the pilot has nothing to steer on; open it before the depth is proven and the pilot no longer fits, so there is no second attempt at the counterbore.
Z_HOLE_D6 mmpinned
6, the final clearance hole. It is opened from Z_PILOT_D with an END MILL and not a reamer - a reamer takes 0.1 to 0.3 and follows the hole it is given, so it can neither remove 0.8 nor move a hole. OPEN: IT MATCHES THE SPACERS, WHICH ARE ALREADY MADE WITH 6 mm CLEARANCE - his, 2026-10-08 - so this does not add float to the stack, it removes a step: the plate was the tight layer at 5.2 while the spacer beneath it was already 6. The cost is head bearing area, 35.2 mm2 down to 28.2, which takes the bearing stress under the head from about 59% to about 73% of 6061-T6 yield at a typical M5 8.8 preload. ESTIMATES, not spec - but the aluminium is the weak member in this joint, not the bolt, so do not torque these to what the fastener would take.
Z_CBORE_WEB4 mmpinned
used by Z_CBORE_DEPTH, Z_BOLT_ENGAGE
4, the plate material left UNDER the head. FLAG: THE COUNTERBORE IS SPECIFIED AS A WEB AND NOT AS A DEPTH, which is the whole point: engagement is bolt minus web minus spacer, so a web target gives the same engagement whatever the plate turns out to measure. A depth dialled from the face does not - T_Z_PLATE is still an unmeasured nominal and the half-inch stock the X end plates came from measured 12.19, a 0.5 swing that is larger than the whole engagement budget. Same thinking as referencing every standing plate from its bottom edge, one layer down. Caliper the plate, subtract this, dial that depth.
Z_CBORE_DEPTH8.7 mmderived
built from T_Z_PLATE - Z_CBORE_WEB
8.7 on the nominal plate, 8.19 if it is really 12.19. DERIVED, so it re-derives when the plate is finally caliper'ed - which is the reason Z_CBORE_WEB is the input and this is the output. It clears M5_HEAD_H by a wide margin, so the head sits several millimetres below the face.
BOLT_Z_CARRIAGE25 mmpart
used by Z_BOLT_ENGAGE
M5 x 25 for all sixteen Z carriage bolts. FLAG: IT WAS M5 x 35 AND THEN BRIEFLY M5 x 30, AND BOTH BOTTOM. The chain moved three times in one day: the counterbore went deeper, the spacer came back 0.2 thinner on a micrometer, and the block's tapped depth turned out to be 8. z-carriage.md carried a headed section called 'Use M5 x 35, not M5 x 30' whose answer is now neither.
Z_BOLT_ENGAGE5.8 mmderived
built from BOLT_Z_CARRIAGE - Z_CBORE_WEB - SPACER_T
5.8 mm into the bearing block, 1.16 x D in STEEL, which is content with about one. FLAG: BUT THE CAP IS 6, NOT 8 - the block threads M5 x 6 - so this sits 0.2 from jamming at the thread's end. A bolt that reaches past the thread reads as tight and never clamps, the same failure BOLT_REACH_MAX exists to prevent on the T-nut joints. OPEN: SPACER_T IS STILL OPEN AND THIS IS BOUNDED BOTH WAYS BY IT: the faced pair must land between 15.0 and 16.0 for a 4 mm web, and if it does not, move the web rather than the bolt. Check this the moment the spacers come off the mill.

Z plate to ball nut, added 2026-10-08 - they were MISSING

BF12_W60.08 mmmeasured
used by BF12_SIDE_CLEAR, BF12_WING_W
60.08 across, HIS CALIPERS 2026-10-08. BF12 is bolted to the X carriage plate on the screw centreline and has to pass between the two Z spacers - see BF12_SIDE_CLEAR. It had been back-derived at 60.2 from his recollection of about 2 mm each side, which was close, but three decisions were resting on it so it got measured.
Z_SPACER_CHANNEL64 mmderived
built from (2 * Z_RAIL_COL) - SPACER_W
used by BF12_SIDE_CLEAR
64, the clear channel between the two spacers' inner edges, which is what BF12 passes through.
BF12_SIDE_CLEAR1.96 mmderived
built from (Z_SPACER_CHANNEL - BF12_W) / 2
1.96 mm each side of BF12. FLAG: HIS CATCH, 2026-10-08, and it nearly sank the rail pitch: bringing the rails in to 114 brings the spacers with them and closes this channel by 1. The new bar being 50 wide rather than 2in gives 0.8 of it back, which lands this within four hundredths of the ~2 mm the as-built carriage has. OPEN: If the spacers are ever ordered at 2in again this drops to 1.5. For more margin than the as-built, a 48 wide bar gives 2.9 and still covers the 44 block by 2 each side.
T_BF12_BLOCK20 mmmeasured
used by Z_NUT_X_1, SCREW_END_IN_BF12
20, BF12's thickness along the screw axis - HIS CALIPERS off a real BF12, 2026-10-08. Not to be confused with BF12_H, which is 43 and is how far the block stands UP its plate from the seating face. This is the one that sets where the Z ball nut can reach: going down, the nut bottoms on BF12's face. OPEN: IT DRIVES NO OTHER DIMENSION - dims.py deps re-derives Z_NUT_X_1 and Z_NUT_X_2 and nothing else, and X and Y get their nut geometry from the end plate chain through SCREW_AXIS_X instead, so do not reach for this symbol on those axes. OPEN: BUT IT IS A TERM IN A BOLT LENGTH, his 2026-10-08: BF12 is face-mounted through its own body, so the 4 x M5 that fix it to the rear plate and to the right X end plate's tongue have to clear this 20 before they reach any thread. Those lengths are not derived in this file yet.
NUT_HOLE_DEPTH16 mmmeasured
16, the drilled depth of a ball nut housing's M5 holes - his, 2026-10-08, against only 8 of thread as supplied. FLAG: ALL FOUR NUTS AND SCREWS ARE THE SAME PART, differing only in length - his, 2026-10-08 - so every figure here applies to X, Y1, Y2 and Z alike. FLAG: THE 8 BELOW THE THREAD IS NOT USABLE TRAVEL: it is tap drill diameter, about 4.2 on an M5 x 0.8, so a 5 mm thread cannot enter it and a longer bolt jams where the mating thread stops, exactly as if the hole ended at 8. That was got wrong once here, so it is written down - chip space is not depth. What the 16 DOES buy is room to tap deeper.
NUT_TAP_ASSUPPLIED8 mmvendor
8, the thread depth every one of the four nut housings arrives with. OPEN: IT IS ENOUGH ON ITS OWN wherever the bolt only has to cross a plate: on the Y sole bracket an M5 x 12 reaches 5.65 through the 1/4in bracket, and the aluminium thread is already stronger than the bolt at that depth. Tapping deeper is margin, not a fix - see NUT_TAP_DEPTH.
NUT_TAP_REACH14 mmmeasured
used by NUT_TAP_DEPTH
14, HOW FAR A BOLT ACTUALLY GOES into the tapped housing - and the measurement is the good part. He had a spare nut, tapped one hole as deep as the tap would go, ran an M5 x 30 in until it bottomed and measured what was left standing out: 16, so the bolt went in 14. FLAG: THAT MEASURES THE CONSEQUENCE, NOT THE ANNOUNCEMENT. The hole drills 16 but a plug tap cannot thread the last couple of millimetres of a blind hole, so the drilled depth was never the usable figure - and reasoning from it is exactly the error that put a wrong bolt in this file twice. One spare part and a bolt settled what two rounds of arithmetic did not. OPEN: IT IS THE TAP'S LIMIT, NOT THE HOLE'S - his, 2026-10-08: that was a plug tap, not a bottoming tap. So another millimetre or two is available if anything ever needs it. Nothing does: M5 x 16 already engages 9.7, which is the 2 x D aluminium asks for.
NUT_TAP_DEPTH12 mmderived
built from NUT_TAP_REACH - 2
12, the working thread depth - NUT_TAP_REACH held back 2 mm so nothing can bottom. FLAG: IT IS MARGIN, NOT A STRENGTH REQUIREMENT, and an earlier version of this note overstated it as 'about half what aluminium asks'. His challenge, 2026-10-08: the load on these four bolts is SHEAR - the screw drives vertically and the bolts run horizontally - so why would thread depth matter? It matters only through preload: a bolted joint carries shear by FRICTION between the faces, and thread is what lets you develop the clamp load that makes the friction. Run the numbers and the joint was never short: at the as-supplied 8 mm thread an M5 reached 5.7, whose aluminium shear area strips at about 11 kN against the bolt's own ~9 kN proof - THE THREAD WAS ALREADY STRONGER THAN THE BOLT, which is what the 2 x D rule of thumb exists to achieve. So the deeper tap buys preload RETENTION, not capacity: aluminium relaxes, and more turns share the load. It costs five minutes in a soft material, which is the only reason it is worth doing at all. OPEN: THE BEARING BLOCKS CANNOT HAVE THIS - their hole is 8.7 against 6 of thread, so nothing to tap into; they are steel and do not need it.
Z_NUT_CBORE_WEB6 mmpinned
used by Z_NUT_CBORE_DEPTH, Z_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE
6, the plate left under the head on N1-N4 - DEEPER than the 4 the C-holes get, and his call 2026-10-08: FLAG: THE 4 mm WEB IS ONLY THE SPACER BOLTS' CONSTRAINT. C1-C16 cross the web AND the 15.2 spacer before they reach the block, so at a 6 web an M5 x 25 would engage 3.8, under a diameter - the 4 is forced there, not chosen. N1-N4 cross only the web and the shim, so they can afford a thicker one, and it buys 2 mm more material under the four heads that sit beneath the lower spindle clamp. OPEN: THE PLATE THEREFORE HAS TWO COUNTERBORE DEPTHS. I had claimed all twenty could share one setting, which was tidier and wrong.
Z_NUT_CBORE_DEPTH6.7 mmderived
built from T_Z_PLATE - Z_NUT_CBORE_WEB
6.7 on the nominal plate, 6.19 if it is really 12.19. Derived off the web for the same reason Z_CBORE_DEPTH is - caliper the plate, subtract the web, dial that depth.
BOLT_Z_NUT16 mmpart
used by BOLT_SOLE_ROOT, Y_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE, Z_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE
M5 x 16 into the ball nut housing - and FLAG: THE SAME BOLT ON EVERY AXIS, because all four nuts are the same part. On Z it crosses a 6 mm web and the shim for 9.7 of engagement; on the Y sole brackets it crosses 6.35 of 1/4in bracket for 9.65. Both need the housing tapped to NUT_TAP_DEPTH first. OPEN: One bolt and one tap setting across four nuts was worth more than the alternative, which was M5 x 12 on Y against the as-supplied 8 mm thread - that works too, and leaves the Y housings untapped, but then two lengths and two tap states are loose in the same build. OPEN: NOT the M5 x 25 that the Z carriage bolts take.
Y_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE9.65 mmderived
built from BOLT_Z_NUT - T_SOLE_BRACKET
9.65 mm into the Y ball nut housing, 1.93 x D, with the housing tapped to NUT_TAP_DEPTH. The sole bracket bolts UP into the inverted nut and there is nothing between them - no spacer, no shim - so this is the simplest of the nut joints: bolt less bracket. OPEN: SAME POINT AS THE Z: the load here is SHEAR and a bolted joint carries shear by friction, so what this joint needs is preload. The four holes are M5 clearance, so a joint that slips gives up lost motion the controller cannot see. Torque them properly and evenly. At the as-supplied 8 mm thread an M5 x 12 would reach 5.65 and the aluminium would still be stronger than the bolt - the deeper tap is margin.
Z_NUT_BOLT_ENGAGE9.7 mmderived
built from BOLT_Z_NUT - Z_NUT_CBORE_WEB - NUT_SHIM
9.7 mm into the nut housing, 1.94 x D in aluminium. It was 5.7 before the housing was tapped deeper, and FLAG: 5.7 WAS ALREADY ENOUGH - the aluminium thread strips at about 11 kN there against the bolt's ~9 kN proof load. The extra is margin against preload relaxation, not capacity. OPEN: WHAT THIS JOINT ACTUALLY NEEDS IS PRELOAD, NOT THREAD. The four bolts are in 6 mm clearance holes on M5, so 0.5 radial float per side - if the joint ever slips, that float becomes up to 1 mm of Z lost motion the controller cannot see. Friction is doing the work: at a modest 2 N.m each the four develop roughly 1.6 kN of friction capacity against a 500 N envelope. TORQUE THEM PROPERLY AND EVENLY; that matters more here than the last few millimetres of thread. Bounded at both ends like Z_BOLT_ENGAGE - too little and the thread strips, more than NUT_TAP_DEPTH and the bolt jams where the thread stops.

the tool, for the 3D view only

TOOL_D6.35 mmnominal
source his, for the model
1/4in, a stand-in cutter. IT IS NOT A MACHINE DIMENSION - nothing is built to it. It exists so the 3D view can show a tool and read out where its tip is, which is the point on the machine an operator actually cares about. His, 2026-10-10.
TOOL_L50.8 mmnominal
source his, for the model
2in of tool standing out of the collet nut, the same stand-in as TOOL_D. DRAWN FROM THE NUT FACE DOWN, so it is stickout and not the cutter's overall length - how much of a real tool is gripped is not something the model can know.
Z_STOP_W50 mmpart
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
50, the block ACROSS the carriage. Cut from 1in 6061, so the piece is 50 x 45 off the slab and no face is machined.
Z_STOP_T25.4 mmpart
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
25.4, the block UP the carriage - the 1in stock thickness, used as it comes. It is the dimension the ø22 bore runs through.
Z_STOP_OUT45 mmpart
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
45, how far the block stands OUT from the carriage plate's front face. The two M5 run in through this, and the counterbore takes 6 of it, which is what leaves 39 of block and puts an M5 x 50 eleven millimetres into the plate.
Z_STOP_FACE_X310 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheets 3a and 3c
310, where the block's LOWER face sits up the carriage plate - and that face is the stop: it is what the Z ball nut lands on. The block runs from here up through Z_STOP_T.
Z_STOP_BORE_D22 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
ø22, the bore the Z screw runs on through, on the screw centreline. FLAG: NOT ø27: a 27 bore reaches 11.5 out from the axis and an M5 clearance hole 10 from the end sits 15 off it, so a 27 would cut into the hole. At 22 the bore wall is 14 out and the ø6 clears it by 1.0.
Z_STOP_BORE_OUT25 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
25, the bore axis out from the carriage plate's face. It is the middle of the 50 across, but NOT the middle of the 45 out - 25 of 45 leaves 20 beyond it, and it has to: the bore then runs 14 to 36 out from the plate, which is the span the ø6 clearance holes are argued against. It lands on the screw axis, which the model reaches independently through SCREW_AXIS_H, also 25.
Z_STOP_BOLT_INSET10 mmpinned

Z_STOP_BOLT_Z12.7 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheets 3a and 3c
The two M5 run in halfway up the 25.4. With the stop face at Z_STOP_FACE_X that puts the tapped pair at X 322.7 on the carriage plate, which is what Sheet 3a drills.
Z_STOP_HOLE_D6 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
ø6 clearance for the two M5, drilled through the 45. It leaves 1.0 of wall to the ø22 bore, so it gets drilled straight.
Z_STOP_CBORE_DEPTH6 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c
6 deep, counterbored from the block's outer face. It is what sets the engagement: 45 less 6 leaves 39 of block, so an M5 x 50 reaches 11 into the carriage plate.
BOLT_Z_STOP50 mmpinned
source machine/drawings/shop-pack.html, Sheet 3c; machine/z-carriage.md
M5 x 50, two of them, holding the stop block to the carriage plate. OPEN: THESE AND THE BLOCK ARE SACRIFICIAL - they fire only if the soft limit and the proximity sensor have both already failed, so do not size them by strength. If one is ever used, replace the block and the bolts.
Z_NUT_X_128 mmderived
built from T_BF12_BLOCK + NUT_INSET_Y
used by Z_NUT_X_2
28. First of the two rows of M5 clearance holes that bolt the Z plate to its ball nut, X from the plate's INSTALLED BOTTOM. FLAG: THESE FOUR HOLES WERE MISSING FROM SHEET 4 ENTIRELY - his catch, 2026-10-08. The plate had sixteen holes to its bearing blocks and none to the nut that drives it. The chain is his: at full down travel the nut bottoms on BF12's face, BF12 is T_BF12_BLOCK thick, so the nut's 40 mm face spans 20 to 60 and the pattern's NUT_INSET_Y of 8 at each end puts the rows at 28 and 52. It self-checks - 8 + 24 + 8 = 40 closes the face, and 28 + NUT_PATTERN_Y = 52 from the other direction. OPEN: Row 1 shares X 28 with C-hole row 1; the nearest pair is 21.5 apart in Y, so they do not foul, but see them on the drawing before the mill.
Z_NUT_X_252 mmderived
built from Z_NUT_X_1 + NUT_PATTERN_Y
52. The second nut row. NUT_PATTERN_Y is 24 and it is the pattern pitch ACROSS the nut's 40 mm face - on the Z plate that face's short axis runs up the plate, which is why the 24 is the X pitch here and the 40 is the Y span.
Z_NUT_Y_162 mmderived
built from (Z_PLATE_W / 2) - (NUT_PATTERN_X / 2)
62.5. First nut column, the 40 mm pattern centred across the plate because the nut is centred on the screw and the screw is on the plate's centreline. Half-millimetre, and that is the price of the odd width - see Z_PLATE_W. These are 5.5 clearance on M5, so it is inside their float.
Z_NUT_Y_2102 mmderived

consistency, settled 2026-10-03

FRONT_STRIP_W50 mmmeasured
used by STRIP_ROW_LOW, STRIP_ROW_HIGH
source his, 2026-10-09
50 - the front joining strip across the seam, identical on X, Y1 and Y2, and also the magnetic tape surface. SETTLED: MEASURED ON THE STRIPS IN HAND 2026-10-14 - they came 1000 x 50 x 3, metric, where this was carried at a nominal 50.8 for 2in bar. THE WARNING BELOW CALLED IT AND THE PRECAUTION PAID: Sheet 8's two rows are dimensioned symmetric about the strip's own centreline and 30 apart, so the narrower strip self-centres on the seam and the row positions simply move in 0.4 to STRIP_ROW_LOW and STRIP_ROW_HIGH. Nothing has to be re-chosen. NOTE: AND THEY NEED NO CUTTING - 1000 is BEAM_LEN exactly. FLAG: IT IS WIDER THAN THE GAP BETWEEN THE BEARING BLOCK ROWS, ON PURPOSE, AND THAT IS THE CHANGE. It used to be 45, chosen as BLOCK_ROW_GAP so the strip sat BETWEEN the block rows with about half a millimetre either side. At 50.8 it runs 2.4 UNDER each block instead, which a thin strip may do: he proved it with 60 x 60 x 4 test pieces that clear with 7.0 of overlap a side. SETTLED: THE Y TOLERANCE GOES FROM ABOUT NOTHING TO ABOUT TEN MILLIMETRES - the next thing in the strip's way is the RAIL, 9.6 from the strip edge - which retires the clash this part used to be gated by. OPEN: 2in STOCK MAY ARRIVE AT 50, NOT 50.8: metric and SAE intermix unpredictably on this supplier's aluminium. It does not matter PROVIDED Sheet 8's two rows are dimensioned symmetric about the strip's own centreline, 30 apart, rather than off an edge - then a narrower strip still self-centres on the seam. Dimension it off an edge and the stock's width tolerance lands straight in the hole positions.
BOX_TONGUE_PROUD60 mmpinned
used by BOX_TONGUE_BOLT_Y, TONGUE_FULL_H
How far the box's laminated tongue stands proud of the top skin, on the back and both sides. The plates sit FLAT ON THE SKIN and the tongue laps them from OUTBOARD, so this is a simple bolted lap - there is no channel and nothing is captured. WAS 61 until 2026-10-03, changed by him for consistency, which makes the bolt line exactly central: BOX_TONGUE_BOLT_Y is 30 above the plate's bottom edge and now also 30 below the tongue's top. Named BOX_TONGUE to keep it clear of TONGUE_H and friends, which are the X end plate's BF12 tongue and a different part entirely.
BOX_TONGUE_BOLT_Y30 mmderived
built from BOX_TONGUE_PROUD / 2
used by XBOX_ROW_Y
The one bolt line at 30 above each plate's bottom edge - side plates and back plate into the box tongues, front fin into its L-brackets. With BOX_TONGUE_PROUD at 60 it is the tongue's mid-height rather than 31 down from a 61. The fin keeps the SAME height even though the part it bolts to changed on 2026-10-03: Sheet 1's T1 and T2 were repurposed from tongue bolts to bracket bolts and did not move, which keeps their 145 mm couple intact. See BOX_FRONT_OVERHANG.

PLATE_H301.625 mmpart
used by FIN_STOCK, SIDE_PLATE_H, REAR_PLATE_W
11 7/8in, THE ONE ORDERED HEIGHT - and his rule 2026-10-08: the two side plates, the back plate and the two front fins ALL stand this high, every hole on them is measured from the BOTTOM edge, and a part that does not measure 301.625 is misordered or mismade and goes back. FLAG: THAT MAKES IT AN ACCEPTANCE CRITERION, NOT A NOMINAL TO DESIGN AROUND, which is why SIDE_PLATE_H, REAR_PLATE_W and FIN_STOCK all derive from it rather than each carrying their own 301.625 - they are one bought size and they cannot be allowed to drift apart in this file. The bottom edge is the reference on all five because it is the edge that bears on the torsion box's top skin, so a common height plus a common reference edge is what makes the beam line up without any part knowing about any other. Check all five against each other on arrival, not just against this number - two parts both wrong by the same amount would still assemble.
SIDE_PLATE_L998.54 mmpart

The seven T-slot stations run 50 to 950 on TSLOT_X_PITCH, so the shortfall sits in the 48.54 of margin past the last hole rather than anywhere load-bearing. Which END the 1.46 comes off is NOT decided here - Sheet 9's X 0 is the plate's left edge and the stations are absolute from it, so if the plate is laid out from the left the slack all lands at the right.

T_BOX_TONGUE24 mmmeasured

FLANGE_LOW_D13.75 mmmeasured

At a 17.5 head the clear channel between the two head rims is 12.5 and the puck DOES NOT FIT; at 13.75 it is 16.25 and it fits. The head diameter was the only variable in that sum. SENSOR_KEEPOUT_SLACK0.625 mmderived

FLAG: THE o9 CLEARANCE HOLES CAN EAT IT: S1-S14 are ø9 on M8 into SLIDING T-nuts, so nothing centres a bolt in its hole and ±0.5 is available. Both rows drifting inboard leaves 0.10 a side. BIAS THE STRIP BOLTS OUTBOARD WHEN TIGHTENING - it costs nothing and it is not a thing to discover after 3 m of one-shot PSA tape is down.
CARRIAGE_TRAVEL820 mmmeasured
used by TRAVEL_LOST_TO_STOPS, TAPE_OVERRUN_END
source his calipers, 2026-10-09
820 mm, THE TRAVEL ON ALL THREE LONG AXES - X, Y1 and Y2 - his measurement 2026-10-09, and it closes an open item the encoder doc had been carrying with stale Mega V XL numbers. FLAG: THE METHOD IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING, because it is not obvious and a future session would otherwise re-derive it badly: put ONE bearing block at each end of the same rail, each pushed until its grease zerk touches the end plate, and measure the INSIDE distance between them. That gap is (rail span) less TWO block bodies - and the real carriage IS two butted blocks, BLOCK_BUTTED, so the gap you measure is exactly the real carriage’s travel rather than a proxy for it. The zerks are necessarily on the blocks’ OUTER ends, since otherwise a pair could not butt, so the test reproduces the real stop geometry at both ends. SETTLED: One figure serves all three beams: butted blocks set the carriage width on X and Y alike and every beam is BEAM_LEN, so there is one carriage length and one rail length. The stops are the X end plates meeting the back plate or a front fin.
TRAVEL_RAIL_LIMITED845.82 mmderived
built from BEAM_LEN - BLOCK_BUTTED
used by TRAVEL_LOST_TO_STOPS
845.82 - what the travel WOULD be if the rail ends alone stopped the carriage. SETTLED: IT IS A CROSS-CHECK, NOT THE FIGURE TO USE: CARRIAGE_TRAVEL is measured at 820 and that is what sizes the tape. The two agree to 25.82, which is TRAVEL_LOST_TO_STOPS - the end plates standing inboard of the rail ends plus the zerks’ own protrusion. OPEN: It takes BEAM_LEN as a stand-in for rail length; the rails are 17 x M5 at 60 pitch, so about a metre, not exactly it. Close enough to confirm the measurement is the right size and nowhere near good enough to replace it.
TRAVEL_LOST_TO_STOPS25.82 mmderived
built from TRAVEL_RAIL_LIMITED - CARRIAGE_TRAVEL
25.82 - what the end plates take off the rail-limited travel, about 13 a side. Recorded because it is the number that moves if a stop face ever changes: the fin gets thicker, the back plate moves, or a zerk is swapped for a flush fitting. Nothing derives from it today.
TAPE_STOCK3000 mmpart
used by TAPE_REMAINING
3 m of 10 mm magnetic strip on a steel band with 3M PSA, in stock - the figure design-can-position-feedback.md has carried since 2026-08-25. FLAG: IT IS THE BINDING CONSTRAINT ON THE WHOLE ENCODER LAYOUT and it is a ONE-SHOT consumable: the PSA does not survive a second lay. See TAPE_REMAINING, which is what is left after four axes.
CARRIER_SECTION_L220 mmpart
used by TAPE_PER_BEAM
source his, 2026-10-09
220 mm, the printed tape carrier’s section length - his 2026-10-09, set by the Bambu P1S’s 256 x 256 bed with four laid side by side. OPEN: 220 IS NOT A ROUND NUMBER PICKED FOR TIDINESS, it is the point where two limits nearly meet: below about 206 the four sections no longer span CARRIAGE_TRAVEL at all, and much above 220 the three beams plus Z no longer come out of TAPE_STOCK. 4 x 205 was considered and rejected - it gives exactly 820, which is the travel, leaving nothing for the Hall array or the tape’s own unusable end poles.
CARRIER_SECTIONS4 mmpart
used by TAPE_PER_BEAM
source his, 2026-10-09
Four printed sections per beam. Four fit the bed in one run at 220 long; five would need them narrower. See CARRIER_W, which is open.
TAPE_PER_BEAM880 mmderived
built from CARRIER_SECTION_L * CARRIER_SECTIONS
used by TAPE_OVERRUN_END, TAPE_THREE_BEAMS
880 mm of tape per long axis. The carrier sections butt end to end, joined by loose keys in blind sockets rather than protruding tenons - a male tenon would be swallowed at each joint and four sections would no longer reach 880.
TAPE_OVERRUN_END30 mmderived
built from (TAPE_PER_BEAM - CARRIAGE_TRAVEL) / 2
used by TAPE_Z
30 mm of tape past each end of travel. FLAG: THE OVERRUN IS NOT SLOP, IT IS LOAD-BEARING TWICE OVER. The AS5311 datasheet gives usable stroke as the active area LESS the 2.0 mm Hall array, with the short poles at either edge of the strip excluded from the active area - so tape equal to travel is always short. And the carriage reaches its hard stops exactly when something has gone wrong, which is the worst moment for the reader to be off the end: a magnetic reader that leaves the tape and returns gives a wrong count and does not complain. At 30 a side the array never approaches an end pole, which retires the end-exclusion question rather than requiring it to be respected.
TAPE_THREE_BEAMS2640 mmderived
built from 3 * TAPE_PER_BEAM
used by TAPE_REMAINING
2640 mm for X, Y1 and Y2. One figure times three because CARRIAGE_TRAVEL is the same on all three.
TAPE_Z295.91 mmderived
built from Z_TRAVEL + (2 * TAPE_OVERRUN_END)
used by TAPE_REMAINING
295.91 for the Z axis, on the same overrun rule. OPEN: THE SOFTEST NUMBER IN THIS CHAIN: Z’s tape runs on the EDGE of the Z gantry plate rather than between rails, because the 1605 screw occupies the centre, and that axis has never been laid out - the encoder doc calls its reader allocation reserved rather than spent. Z_TRAVEL is firm; how much tape the edge mounting actually wants is not.
TAPE_REMAINING64.09 mmderived
built from TAPE_STOCK - TAPE_THREE_BEAMS - TAPE_Z
64.09 mm left of the 3 m roll after all four axes. FLAG: THAT IS THE MARGIN ON A ONE-SHOT CONSUMABLE AND IT IS THIN. It is why CARRIER_SECTION_L stops at 220: at 230 the sections come to 2760 for the three beams and the roll goes NEGATIVE once Z is counted. Any of these going up - a longer travel on one axis, a more generous overrun, a wider Z allowance - comes out of 64 mm. OPEN: Buy more tape before changing any of them, not after.
FLANGE_HALF8.75 mmderived
built from FLANGE_NORMAL_D / 2
8.65, the M8 equivalent of FLANGE_M5_HALF. Same reason: this is what an edge distance has to clear. FLAG: IT IS THE BEAM-END HEAD'S HALF, off FLANGE_NORMAL_D's 17.3 - the bolt that goes into a beam's corner bores, which his rule 2026-10-08 keeps at the old style. OPEN: THE FRONT STRIP'S BOLT IS A DIFFERENT PART at 13.8, so its half is 6.9: use FLANGE_LOW_D for anything on that joint. This symbol briefly read 6.9 on 2026-10-08 when the strip bolt was wrongly written into FLANGE_NORMAL_D, which would have relaxed every edge distance in the repo on the strength of a bolt that is not in those joints.
FLANGE_DRIVE0 mmpart
NOT A LENGTH - a fact parked where the other fastener facts live, because it changes how a joint is assembled. Both the M8 and M5 flange fasteners are FLANGED BUTTON HEADS DRIVEN BY A HEX SOCKET in the centre of the dome, settled 2026-10-03. Two consequences. (1) They need AXIAL access for a hex key and cannot be driven by a spanner, so anywhere a bolt sits in a narrow slot the key reaches it where a wrench never would - the front strip's rows, 45 between the bearing block rows, are the case in point. (2) The whole head is FLANGE_NORMAL_H or FLANGE_M5_H proud and no more; nothing sits above the dome.
RAIL_BOLT_HOLE_D6 mmvendor
6, the rail's through-hole - Vevor HGH block page, column d. FLAG: THE 0.5 OF RADIAL FLOAT ON AN M5 IS NOT SLOP, IT IS THE MECHANISM. It is what lets a bolt tilt to catch a T-nut thread that is not perfectly aligned, and what lets the rail be nudged into line. He found an M6 would enter but not wiggle, and concluded the bolts would have to be pre-threaded into their T-nuts and slid in one at a time - 51 assemblies by hand. That difficulty WAS the symptom: a mounting hole with no clearance is the wrong bolt, not a hard job.
BOLT_RAIL16 mmvendor
M5 x 16 for every rail mounting bolt - Vevor HGH block page. Socket head 8.5 across, which seats in the 9.5 counterbore with 0.5 to spare where an M6's 10.0 will not go in at all.
RAIL_CBORE_D9.5 mmvendor
9.5, the counterbore along an HGR20 rail - Vevor HGH block page, column D, and he confirms it on the part. Was carried as 10. FLAG: IT IS WHAT SETTLED THE M5-versus-M6 ARGUMENT: the manual contradicts itself, the block page giving M5 x 16 with D 9.5 / h 8.5 / d 6 and the rail page giving S = M6 x 1P. An M6 SOCKET HEAD IS 10.0 ACROSS AND CANNOT SIT IN A 9.5 COUNTERBORE - he tried it. The block page is right, the rail page is an error, and Sheet 3a's fourteen M5 holes stand as drawn.
RAIL_CBORE_DEPTH8.5 mmvendor
8.5, the counterbore depth - Vevor HGH block page, column h, confirmed on the part. Was carried as 10, which also made the rail look like half its 17.5 height. It is one term in how far a hex driver has to reach to a rail bolt through the Z plate and spacer.
RAIL_CBORE_FIRST20 mmpart
used by RAIL_CBORE_N_BEAM, RAIL_CBORE_N_Z
First counterbore from either end of a rail - the pattern is symmetric, 20 in from both ends with RAIL_CBORE_PITCH between.
RAIL_CBORE_PITCH60 mmpart
used by RAIL_CBORE_N_BEAM, RAIL_CBORE_N_Z
Counterbore pitch along the rail.
RAIL_CBORE_N_BEAM17 mmderived
built from ((BEAM_LEN - (2 * RAIL_CBORE_FIRST)) / RAIL_CBORE_PITCH) + 1
17 on a 1000 rail. IT COMES OUT EXACTLY, which is the check that the 20 / 60 / 20 pattern is right: gantry-beam-joint.md has said 'HGR20, 17 x M5 at 60 mm' since the bench measurements, from a completely separate direction.
RAIL_CBORE_N_Z7 mmderived

Z_RAIL_L400 mmpart
used by Z_TRAVEL, RAIL_CBORE_N_Z
Z rail length, on a 407 carriage plate - which is where the 7 mm at one end that z-carriage.md calls 'forced, not chosen' comes from.
BLOCK_OVERHANG_CLEAR6 mmmeasured
Clear height under the HGH20 block, above the surface the rail sits on. His, 2026-10-03, stated as AT MOST 6 - so it is an upper bound, and anything designed against it should leave room. FLAG: WAS 4.5 from the 2026-09-29 bench table. The 6 is the better number because IT PREDICTS BOTH OF HIS MEASURED OUTCOMES, where 4.5 only predicted one: a 4 mm strip stacks to 5 and fits, a 1/4in strip stacks to 7.35 and does not. At 4.5 the 4 mm case came out marginal on paper while fitting in practice, which was the tell that the clearance figure was wrong rather than the fit. IT IS THE CEILING ON ANYTHING THAT REACHES UNDER THE BLOCK.
STRIP_HEAD_REACH21.875 mmderived
built from RAIL_SLOT_INSET + (FLANGE_LOW_D / 2)
How far a strip bolt's flange head reaches from the beam's seam: SETTLED: 21.9 on the 13.8 head adopted 2026-10-08, against a strip half-width of 22.5. THE HEAD NOW STOPS 0.6 INSIDE THE STRIP'S OWN EDGE and reaches nothing. OPEN: THAT RETIRES A WHOLE CLEARANCE QUESTION rather than changing its answer: at the old 17.3 head this was 23.65, past the 22.5 and past a block inner edge near 23, so the head's RIM passed under the bearing block's overhang by about half a millimetre. That was fine and it was MEASURED - he checked it 2026-10-03 on a real beam with rails and blocks fitted, the rim being under a millimetre thick at that radius - and the 3D model only flagged it because it draws the block as a plain solid with no overhang relief. None of that applies now; nothing goes under the block. See STRIP_STACK, which existed to bound it.
STRIP_STACK4 mmderived
built from T_FRONT_STRIP + FLANGE_RIM_H
What has to pass under the block where the bolt head reaches past the strip's edge: the strip plus the flange AT ITS RIM, which is the only part that gets that far out. 5 against BLOCK_OVERHANG_CLEAR's 6, so it CLEARS BY 1 - and both of those are upper bounds, so the real margin is larger. SETTLED: Measured on a real beam at 4 mm. At the old 6.35 the stack was 7.35 against the same 6 and it did NOT fit, which is what drove the strip to 4. The arithmetic and the measurement now agree in both directions; with the old 4.5 clearance they did not, and the measurement was right. OPEN: KEPT, BUT IT NO LONGER BITES: on the 13.8 head adopted 2026-10-08 STRIP_HEAD_REACH is 21.9 against a 22.5 strip half-width, so NO PART OF THE HEAD REACHES THE STRIP'S EDGE, let alone under the block. This stays because the strip thickness argument it records is why T_FRONT_STRIP is 4 and not 1/4in, and that reasoning is still load-bearing.
T_FRONT_STRIP3 mmmeasured
used by STRIP_STACK, STRIP_BOLT_REACH
source his measurement of the strips in hand, 2026-10-14
3 - the front joining strip, all three beams, MEASURED ON THE STRIPS IN HAND. SETTLED: THEY ARRIVED METRIC - 1000 x 50 x 3, his reading 2026-10-14. Ordered as 1/8in 6061 flat bar and the file carried 3.175 while warning it might come as 4.0; it came as neither, which is the fifth nominal on this machine to turn out metric. SETTLED: THEY NEED NO CUTTING - 1000 is BEAM_LEN exactly, where the note here used to say 4ft stock cut to length. SETTLED: BLOCK CLEARANCE IS THE SAFE END: STRIP_STACK is 4.0, against BLOCK_OVERHANG_CLEAR's 6, and that is better than either figure this was carried at. FLAG: THE BOLT IS WHERE IT BITES AND THIS IS THE TIGHTEST OF THE THREE CASES - STRIP_BOLT_SLACK is 0.2, against 0.375 at 3.175 and 1.2 at 4.0, because a thinner strip lets the same M8 x 10 reach FURTHER into the T-slot. It still fits; see STRIP_BOLT_REACH, which is now tracked rather than argued in prose. FLAG: WAS 1/4in (6.35) UNTIL 2026-10-03 AND THAT DID NOT FIT: the bolt heads fouled the bearing blocks, confirmed on the real beam. The strip is NOT T_BACK_JOIN_PLATE - they shared 6.35 once and are different parts with different constraints; the back plate is sized for stiffness, the strip by what clears a block overhang. See gantry-beam-joint.md for the stiffness given up.
BOLT_FULL_ENGAGE8.3 mmderived
built from TSLOT_LIP_T + TNUT_THREAD_M8
8.3 - how far a bolt must reach PAST A PLATE to take all the thread a T-nut has: across the lip, then the nut's full 6. SETTLED: WITH BOLT_REACH_MAX IT IS A WINDOW RATHER THAN A LIMIT, 8.3 to 9.5 past the plate, and its width is exactly TNUT_BACK_CLEAR. So the right bolt for a plate of thickness t is between t + 8.3 and t + 9.3, and under t + 8.3 the joint is simply giving away engagement. FLAG: IT IS THE SAME NUMBER AS TNUT_BACK_FACE and that is not a coincidence - full engagement means the bolt tip reaches the nut's back face. It carries its own name because the two answer different questions and a reader looking for bolt length will not go looking at where a nut's back face sits.
STRIP_BOLT_REACH7 mmderived
built from BOLT_FRONT_STRIP - T_FRONT_STRIP
used by STRIP_BOLT_ENGAGE, STRIP_BOLT_SLACK
7 - how far the front strip's M8 x 10 reaches PAST the strip. It follows the strip thickness, so a thinner strip reaches further.
STRIP_BOLT_ENGAGE4.7 mmderived
built from STRIP_BOLT_REACH - TSLOT_LIP_T
4.7 - the thread the front strip's M8 x 10 actually engages: its reach past the strip, less the lip it crosses first. Against TNUT_THREAD_M8's 6 available, so it is giving away 1.3. FLAG: THIS IS THE FIGURE THAT WAS WRONG IN PROSE AND IS NOW EXPRESSED - the sheets and fasteners.toml compared reach past the plate against a 7.2 ceiling, which double-counted the lip and made a 10 look nearly too long when it is actually SHORT. OPEN: AN M8 x 12 PUTS IT AT 6.0, the full thread, with 0.3 still clear of the slot floor - inside the BOLT_FULL_ENGAGE window where the 10 is under it. HIS CALL, and the head is unchanged so nothing about block clearance moves.
STRIP_BOLT_SLACK2.3 mmderived
built from BOLT_REACH_MAX - STRIP_BOLT_REACH
2.3 - what is left before the front strip's bolt bottoms on the slot floor. SETTLED: IT IS NOT TIGHT, and it read 0.2 for one commit on the wrong BOLT_REACH_MAX. Bottoming is not the constraint on this joint; engagement is - see STRIP_BOLT_ENGAGE.

T-slot bolt lengths, 2026-10-04

TSLOT_LIP_T2.3 mmmeasured
used by BOLT_FULL_ENGAGE, STRIP_BOLT_ENGAGE, TNUT_BACK_FACE
Thickness of the T-slot lip on the 30-6060 - the shoulder a slide-in T-nut bears against, so it is also HOW DEEP the nut's back face starts. His calipers, 2026-10-04. The 8020 section drawing calls the lip 4.20, which is a DIFFERENT dimension (the lip's reach inward, not its thickness) and must not be substituted here. This is one of the two numbers that decide bolt length; nothing in the repo had it before.
TSLOT_DEPTH9.3 mmmeasured
used by TNUT_BACK_CLEAR, BOLT_REACH_MAX
9.3 - beam face to T-slot FLOOR on the 30-6060. SETTLED: HIS CALIPERS AGAIN 2026-10-14, re-taken while the bolt-length error was being corrected, and it replaces the 9.5 he read on 2026-10-04. The 0.2 matters here in a way it would not elsewhere: it comes straight off TNUT_BACK_CLEAR, which is the whole width of the bolt-length window between BOLT_FULL_ENGAGE and BOLT_REACH_MAX, so the window narrows from 1.2 to 1.0. Original note: the other half of the bolt-length calculation, and the figure the published section does not give. The other half of the bolt-length calculation, and the figure the published section does not give: the drawing dimensions the slot's mouth (8.14) and channel width (16.51) but never its depth, which is why bolt lengths could not be computed before this was taken.
TSLOT_MOUTH_W8.14 mmpart
source manufacturer-assets/8020-30-6060-Profile-Drawing.jpg
8.14, the T-slot's MOUTH on the 30-6060 - the gap between the two lips, which is what you see looking at the face. It was already in the repo as prose inside TSLOT_DEPTH's note and nowhere else, so nothing could draw a slot from it. It is NOT the channel width behind the lips, which the same drawing gives as 16.51, and it is not a bolt clearance - an M8 passes the mouth with room because the lip, not the mouth, is what a T-nut bears on.
TNUT_BODY_T6 mmmeasured
used by TNUT_BACK_FACE
8020 13025 T-nut body thickness where the thread runs - 6, his calipers 2026-10-04, confirming the published F = 6.00. THIS IS THE CEILING ON THREAD ENGAGEMENT for every bolt-on plate on every beam: the bolt cannot engage more than 6 mm no matter how long it is, so engagement is set by the nut and not by the bolt. The nut's total height is 7.80 including its 1.80 boss, and that boss is not threaded usefully - do not compute engagement off 7.80.
TNUT_BACK_FACE8.3 mmderived
built from TSLOT_LIP_T + TNUT_BODY_T
used by TNUT_BACK_CLEAR
Where the T-nut's back face sits below the beam face: 8.3. The boss (1.80) is SHORTER than the lip (2.3), so the nut's shoulders bear on the lip underside and the boss top sits 0.5 recessed from the face - which means the lip thickness, not the boss, is what sets this depth.
TNUT_BACK_CLEAR1 mmderived
built from TSLOT_DEPTH - TNUT_BACK_FACE
Air behind the T-nut, between its back face and the slot floor: 1.2. THIS IS THE PROTRUSION BUDGET - how far a bolt tip may stand proud of the nut before it bottoms on the slot floor. A bolt that bottoms reads as a tight bolt that never pulls the plate down, which is the failure this symbol exists to prevent.
BOLT_REACH_MAX9.3 mmalias
another name for TSLOT_DEPTH
used by STRIP_BOLT_SLACK
9.5 - the most a bolt may reach past the back face of any plate bolted to a beam before it BOTTOMS ON THE SLOT FLOOR. Bolt length minus plate thickness must not exceed this. FLAG: IT WAS 7.2 AND THAT WAS WRONG - his catch 2026-10-14. The old expression was TNUT_BODY_T + TNUT_BACK_CLEAR, which is the reach measured from the T-NUT'S FRONT FACE, and it was then compared against reach past the PLATE. Those two faces are TSLOT_LIP_T apart: a bolt crosses the lip, and the nut's boss inside it, before it meets any usable thread. Measured from the plate's back face the stack is 2.3 of lip, then 6 of thread to TNUT_BACK_FACE, then TNUT_BACK_CLEAR of air to the floor at 9.3. FLAG: THE OLD FIGURE RULED OUT BOLTS THAT FIT - this note itself said M8 x 12 was out on a 4 mm strip at a reach of 8.0, when 8.0 is 1.5 clear of the floor and gives 5.7 of the 6 available thread. Anything chosen against the 7.2 is too SHORT rather than too long, and short costs engagement. SETTLED: USE THE WINDOW, NOT THE CEILING: see BOLT_FULL_ENGAGE - a bolt wants to reach between 8.3 and 9.3 past the plate, and the window's width is exactly TNUT_BACK_CLEAR - 1.0 on the re-measured slot depth.
T_OUTBOARD_PLATE3.39 mmalias
another name for T_SIDE_PLATE
ALIAS OF T_SIDE_PLATE, collapsed 2026-10-07 - it is the same piece of steel under two names, the Y outboard plate which on Y IS the back joining plate. It had its own value of 3.175 and fed NOTHING, while T_SIDE_PLATE carried the identical nominal and fed sixteen symbols. FLAG: THE TRAP WAS WHERE THE MEASUREMENT WOULD LAND: the standing instruction to measure the delivered sheet named THIS symbol, so a caliper reading written here would have changed nothing - not the rear plate's length, not the box width, not Sheet 7's hole columns. Put the reading in T_SIDE_PLATE. Same failure as T_DOUBLER the same day: two symbols for one part, only one of them wired up. Kept rather than deleted because the prose refers to it. Its bolt-reach consequence lives in BOLT_BACK_Y and BOLT_REACH_MAX, and SETTLED: THE ORDER SETTLED IT COMFORTABLY: at the ordered 10 gauge (3.429) an M8 x 10 reaches 6.571 against the 7.2 limit and clears by 0.629. This note used to say 0.24 at an 11-gauge 3.04, which was inside caliper noise and is why BOLT_BACK_Y_WASHER was specified; the washer stays, but for load spreading rather than for reach.
BOLT_FRONT_STRIP10 mmpinned
used by STRIP_BOLT_REACH
M8 x 10 for the front joining strip, all three beams - 42 of them, and these are the ones that must be A4 / 316 austenitic because the magnetic tape runs between their rows. M8 x 12 IS WRONG HERE: it reaches 8.0 against BOLT_REACH_MAX's 7.2 and bottoms before the head seats. OPEN: THE x 10 IS NO LONGER COMFORTABLE AND THAT IS NEW: it was chosen against a 4 mm strip, where reach is exactly TNUT_BODY_T's 6 with 1.2 spare to the slot floor. T_FRONT_STRIP is now open between 3.175 and 4.0, and at 3.175 the reach is 6.825 - inside the limit by 0.375, which is caliper noise. FLAG: IF THE STOCK CALIPERS AT 1/8in, DECIDE THIS BEFORE TIGHTENING ANYTHING: an M8 x 8, or a washer under the head the way BOLT_BACK_Y_WASHER solves the identical problem on the 3.175 back plate. A washer here must be A4 / 316 as well, for the same reason the bolts are.
BOLT_BACK_X12 mmpinned
M8 x 12 for the X back joining plate, 6.35 mm 6061 - 28 of them. Reach 5.65, so 5.65 of the available 6 engaged and 1.55 spare to the floor. The next size up, M8 x 16, would reach 9.65 and bottom.
BOLT_BACK_Y_WASHER1.6 mmnominal
A plain M8 washer under the head on the Y back plate - NOT a stiffness or clearance figure, a shim that buys margin. SETTLED: THE MARGIN IT WAS BOUGHT FOR TURNED OUT NOT TO BE NEEDED, and the washer stays anyway: at the ORDERED 10 gauge (T_SIDE_PLATE 3.429) an M8 x 10 bare reaches 6.571 against the 7.2 of BOLT_REACH_MAX, clearing by 0.629 - outside caliper noise, where the old figures were not. With the washer the reach is 4.971 and 2.229 spare. OPEN: THIS NOTE USED TO READ 'only 3.175 nominal and possibly 3.04, so M8 x 10 bare leaves 0.24-0.37', which was the whole reason for the washer and is now stale in both numbers. It is kept for the reason it was wanted anyway: it spreads load on 3.4 mm steel. 56 washers, two beams.
BOLT_BACK_Y10 mmpinned
M8 x 10 for the Y back plate - the outboard steel plate, 28 per beam, 56 total - WITH the washer in BOLT_BACK_Y_WASHER. Same length as the front strip's bolts, so the buy is two lengths and not three: 98 of M8 x 10 (42 A4 / 316 for the front strips, 56 plain for the Y backs) and 28 of M8 x 12 for the X back. 126 bolts against 126 T-nuts.

the ball screws

SCREW_D16 mmpart
Ball screw nominal diameter - the 16 in 1605. All four axes run the same screw.
SCREW_LEAD5 mmpart
source Vevor DSG1605 page, printed manual with the ball screw kit
5, the lead - how far the nut travels in one turn of the screw. It is the 05 in 1605 and in DSG1605, so it has been in every reference to the part from the start, but it was never a symbol and nothing could be drawn or computed from it. RIGHT-HAND, his 2026-10-10, which the paperwork does not state either. It is the lead and not a pitch: a 1605 is single-start, so the two happen to be the same number here, and they would not be on a multi-start screw.
SCREW_JOURNAL_D10 mmpart
The bare shaft at each end, turned down from the 16 to ride in its bearing. Both ends are the same diameter; only the LENGTHS differ.
SCREW_JOURNAL_BF11 mmpart
used by SCREW_END_IN_BF12, SCREW_BARE_TOTAL
Bare shaft at the BF12 end - the short one. It only has to reach through the bearing.
SCREW_JOURNAL_CAST54.5 mmpart
used by CASTING_INBOARD_Y, SCREW_BARE_TOTAL
Bare shaft at the stepper casting end. Long because the coupler lives on it as well as the bearing, which is also why the casting end is the one that sets how far the screw stands off its mount.
SCREW_BARE_TOTAL65.5 mmderived
built from SCREW_JOURNAL_BF + SCREW_JOURNAL_CAST
used by SCREW_THREAD_LONG, SCREW_THREAD_Z
Both journals together, 65.5. It is what comes off an overall length to leave thread, and it is the same on every axis.
SCREW_OAL_LONG1000 mmpart
used by SCREW_REAR_END_Y, SCREW_THREAD_LONG
Overall length of the X, Y1 and Y2 screws.
SCREW_OAL_Z400 mmpart
used by SCREW_THREAD_Z
Overall length of the Z screw.
SCREW_THREAD_LONG934.5 mmderived
built from SCREW_OAL_LONG - SCREW_BARE_TOTAL
Threaded portion on X, Y1 and Y2 - 934.5. This, not the overall 1000, is the most the nut could ever traverse, and the travel is shorter again by the nut's own length and both stops.
SCREW_THREAD_Z334.5 mmderived
built from SCREW_OAL_Z - SCREW_BARE_TOTAL
Threaded portion on Z - 334.5, against a 400 rail.
SCREW_AXIS_H25 mmpart
used by Y_SCREW_BELOW_BEAM
Screw centreline above the seating face of whatever carries it. SETTLED: CONFIRMED off the BF/BK12 spec by him, 2026-10-10: the ball screw centreline is 25 above the bottom of the block. That makes it the FIXED point in the Y height chain and puts the disagreement on the nut side - see NUT_AXIS_TO_FACE. BF12 at one end and the stepper casting at the other must both put the axis here or the screw is not straight - NUT_AXIS_TO_FACE's note already records that BK-12 and BF-12 share an h of 25, and this is that number as a symbol. It is also his 25 for the Z top stop block's bore, seen from the carriage plate, and footnote 3.8's "25 for the casting's axis" in the X nut chain.
NUT_BODY_L40 mmpart
used by NUT_OAL
Ball nut body along the screw, without its flange. His 2026-10-10. Numerically the same as NUT_FACE_Y's L, which is the mounting flat's length - they are different dimensions that happen to agree, the same trap NUT_FACE_Y's own note describes between L and H.
NUT_FLANGE_T14 mmpart
used by NUT_OAL
The flange bolted to the nut, measured along the screw. His 2026-10-10.
NUT_OAL54 mmderived
built from NUT_BODY_L + NUT_FLANGE_T
Ball nut overall along the screw, 54. This is the figure that eats travel at each end, not the 40.
NUT_W52 mmpart
Ball nut across, 52 - the same figure as NUT_FACE_X's B, which is why SOLE_BRACKET_L closes at the screw axis plus 26.

BK12, the casting, and the motors

BK12_W60 mmpart
BK12 across - the same 60 as BF12 and as the beam, which is what lets SCREW_AXIS_X treat the (EXT_W / 2) term as by-construction rather than an assumption.
BK12_L30 mmpart
BK12 along the screw axis. BF12's equivalent is T_BF12_BLOCK at 20, so the fixed end is 10 longer than the free end.
BK12_H43 mmpart
43 - a real BK12’s centre boss, the tallest part of it. FLAG: IT WAS GIVEN AS 45 FIRST AND IS 43: measured off a real BK12 and confirmed in the Vevor spec sheet, his 2026-10-10. If a 45 appears anywhere it is stale. SETTLED: It is the same 43 as BF12_H, which is what two supports carrying one screw ought to come to. OPEN: DO NOT READ IT ONTO THE CASTING: the cast stepper frame’s screw end is BK12-SHAPED but its boss is CASTING_BOSS_H at 44, a millimetre over, and both figures come off real parts.
CASTING_FOOTPRINT_L77 mmpart
used by INTERPOSER_PROUD, CASTING_OAL
77 - the cast stepper frame's bolted footprint along the screw, front flange of the motor mount to the front edge of the casting. His calipers off the casting's underside, 2026-10-10. FLAG: IT WAS 72.5 AND THE 72.5 WAS WRONG, remeasured after the 3D view put an M8 flange head 0.85 from the casting's face. Sheet 3a's K1-K6 sit in it. The motor frame starts immediately after this. SETTLED: ON A 150 BAR IT LEAVES 73 EXPOSED, which is what the interposer's two T-nut columns have to live in.
CASTING_FOOTPRINT_W60 mmpart
used by CASTING_WING_W
The casting's footprint across, matching BK12_W and the beam.
CASTING_RAIL_H30 mmpart
used by CASTING_RAIL_BOLT_L, CAST_HEAD_Z_UP
The rails that carry the motor frame off the BK12 section are 30 high. They are the open part of the casting - the model draws them as solid, which is conservative for an interference check and wrong for a photograph.
MOTOR_FRAME_T12.5 mmpart
used by CASTING_OAL
The casting's NEMA 23 mounting plate, measured along the screw. It begins where CASTING_FOOTPRINT_L ends.
MOTOR_FRAME_W60 mmpart
The motor frame is 60 square - wider than the 56 motor it carries.
MOTOR_BODY_SQ56 mmpart
NEMA 23 body, square section. These are the integrated closed-loop units, so the body carries its own driver and is longer than a plain NEMA 23.
MOTOR_BODY_L94 mmpart
used by CASTING_PLUS_MOTOR
NEMA 23 body length. OPEN: HIS FIRST FIGURE WAS 99 AND HE CORRECTED IT TO 94 the same day - if 99 appears anywhere it is stale.
CASTING_OAL89.5 mmderived
built from CASTING_FOOTPRINT_L + MOTOR_FRAME_T
used by CASTING_PLUS_MOTOR
The casting alone along the screw, 85 - footprint plus the motor plate.
CASTING_PLUS_MOTOR183.5 mmderived
built from CASTING_OAL + MOTOR_BODY_L
179 from the casting's leading edge to the back of the motor. This is the figure that decides whether a stepper end fouls anything, and it is why the motors are worth drawing at all - the casting on its own clears everything easily.
BF12_WING_H30 mmpart
used by TONGUE_MIN
30 - the height of BF12’s two SIDE WINGS, the low flanges either side of the centre boss, and the only part of the block that anything bolts to. His, 2026-10-10. The four mounting holes live here, at BF12_BASE_TO_ROW1 and that plus BF12_ROW_PITCH off the base - 7 and 25 - so a 30 wing clears the upper row by 5. SETTLED: THIS IS WHAT THE TONGUE HAS TO BACK, not BF12_H’s 43: that 43 is the centre boss carrying the bearing and it stands above the wings touching nothing. OPEN: An earlier version of this note had the right 30 for the wrong reason - it guessed the block “may stand proud above its top row” rather than knowing it is a T-section. Same number, but one of those is checkable against the part and the other is not.

the T-sections, BF12 and the casting end

BF12_BOSS_W34 mmpart

CASTING_BOSS_W34 mmpart

Y_SCREW_BELOW_BEAM35 mmderived
built from T_INTERPOSER + SCREW_AXIS_H
used by NUT_FACE_BELOW_BEAM
35 - the Y screw centreline below the Y beam's underside, and HIS FIGURE STATED DIRECTLY on 2026-10-10: ten of interposer, twenty-five to the bore. It exists as a symbol because it had been an assembly three symbols deep that nobody could check - the registry held SCREW_AXIS_X and X_SCREW_AXIS and nothing at all for Y's height, so a disagreement of 8 between this and the ball nut chain went unnoticed until the 3D model needed to place a screw. A number the model needs and the registry cannot state is a gap whether or not it is wrong.
XEP_CUT245 mmpart
used by XEP_E_COL_HIGH, XEP_CUT_CHECK, XEP_WASTE_L, TONGUE_H, TONGUE_BF12_ROW1_BARE, XEP_BEAM_TOP_X
245 - what the X gantry end plate is CUT to, as drawn on Sheet 2a, out of a 300 piece. XEP_H's floor plus XEP_SOLE_TAB: the plate reaches 10 past the nut plane so the sole plate's base has a face to sit against. FLAG: ITS BOTTOM EDGE IS NOT THE NUT PLANE - every coordinate up the plate carries XEP_SOLE_TAB. FLAG: A SEPARATE SYMBOL FROM XEP_H ON PURPOSE. XEP_H is the floor the plate must reach and moves whenever the nut or the interposer moves; the cut is a decision about a piece of metal and should move only when somebody decides to cut differently. XEP_CUT_CHECK enforces the relationship.

the tongue bolt's outboard end

FENDER_WASHER_D25 mmnominal
M8 fender washer, outside diameter. OPEN: A CATALOGUE FIGURE AND NOT HIS - caliper the washers actually bought. It spreads the clamp into 19 mm plywood, so the diameter is the whole point of choosing a fender over a plain washer; a plain M8 washer is about 16 and would be most of the way to pulling through.
FENDER_WASHER_T1.1 mmmeasured
1.1 - M8 fender washer thickness. SETTLED: HIS CALIPER, 2026-10-12, off the washers in hand, and two of them stack to 2.2. That closes the OPEN this symbol carried and closes footnote 7.9, which had asked for exactly this figure before a box of bolts was bought. FLAG: IT IS 0.9 THINNER THAN THE CATALOGUE 2 IT REPLACED, AND THE REAR PLATE'S M8 x 45 NO LONGER FITS. That joint runs through TWO of these, so the stack lost 1.8 and the bolt protrudes 1.8 further: 45 less the 33.55 of grip is 11.45, against an ACORN_NUT_DEPTH of 11. It bottoms on the dome and the joint never clamps - which is the precise failure the 11 was measured to rule out. The side plates go the other way and are fine: one washer, 40 less 29.529 is 10.471, inside the 11, and engagement caps at ACORN_NUT_THREAD's 10 rather than the old 9.571. SETTLED: THE REAR PLATE TAKES A THIRD WASHER AND KEEPS ITS M8 x 45 - his call 2026-10-12, so the stack lifts the nut 3.3, it protrudes 10.35 inside the 11, and it engages the full 10 of acorn thread. See the rear-plate-to-tongue joint in fasteners.toml.
ACORN_NUT_D15 mmnominal
M8 acorn nut across the corners - 13 across the flats, about 15 across the points. OPEN: CATALOGUE, NOT MEASURED. Drawn round in the model because a hexagon buys nothing at this scale.
ACORN_NUT_H13 mmnominal
M8 acorn nut overall height. OPEN: CATALOGUE, NOT MEASURED - but the question this note carried is CLOSED: the nuts are in hand and the figures that matter are ACORN_NUT_THREAD and ACORN_NUT_DEPTH, both measured 2026-10-11. The overall height was never a substitute for them and still is not.
ACORN_NUT_THREAD10 mmmeasured
10 - the thread a bolt can engage in the M8 acorn nut. SETTLED: HIS MEASUREMENT 2026-10-11, OFF TWO DIFFERENT BRANDS IN HAND, and both give the same 10. That is 1.25 x D, so a bolt that reaches it is properly held. FLAG: A BOLT CANNOT ENGAGE MORE THAN THIS NO MATTER HOW LONG IT IS - the extra goes into ACORN_NUT_DEPTH's spare room and then stops dead. tools/make-fasteners.py caps engagement here the same way it caps a T-nut's.
ACORN_NUT_DEPTH11 mmmeasured
11 - how much BOLT the M8 acorn nut will take before it bottoms on the closed end. SETTLED: HIS MEASUREMENT 2026-10-11, the same on both brands. FLAG: THIS IS A HARD CEILING ON BOLT LENGTH, not a guideline: past it the bolt lands on the dome and the joint never clamps, however hard the nut is turned. It is the figure Sheet 7's footnote 7.9 asked for. The two acorn joints both clear it - the rear plate's 40 protrudes 6.65 and the side plates' 40 protrudes 9.571 - and it says the rear plate's 6.65 CANNOT be improved by going longer: a 45 would want 11.65 and bottom out.

TT_BED_SPACER5.29 mmderived

SETTLED: TAKEN UP BY ONE 5 mm M5-BORE ALUMINIUM SPACER PER SCREW - his, 2026-10-11, and he can buy them, which is what settled it over the washer stack considered first. TT_BED_END_BOLT5 mmmeasured
M5 - the screws into the tee track sections' ends, TWO PER END. SETTLED: HE DRILLED AND TAPPED THEM 2026-10-11, so this is a finished hole and not a plan: 4.2 drill, M5 tap, TT_BED_END_ENGAGE of thread. FLAG: M4 WAS RECOMMENDED AND M4 WAS WRONG. The Mega V used two M4 SELF-TAPPING screws per end - not machine screws in a tapped hole, which this file called them until he corrected it - and the advice was to drill 3.3 and tap M4 on the reasoning that three in-and-out cycles is light duty. His measurement killed it: A 3.3 DRILL ENTERED THE EXISTING HOLE WITH NO RESISTANCE, so the hole was already at or above the M4 tapping size and an M4 tap would have had almost nothing to cut. THE CYCLE COUNT WAS THE WRONG THING TO JUDGE IT ON; the hole size was, and it was measurable all along.

FLAG: THE PART IS 5 AND THE GAP IS TT_BED_SPACER'S 5.29 - they are different numbers on purpose, and the difference is TT_BED_FLOAT. Do not 'correct' either to match the other.

0.29 per end, 0.58 across the bed - how far the bed can shift before a spacer touches. TT_BED_END_ENGAGE15 mmmeasured
used by TT_BED_END_SCREW_L
15 of M5 thread in each panel end - his, 2026-10-11, measured off the tapped hole. SETTLED: THAT IS 3 x D AND IT CLOSES THE LAST WORRY ABOUT THIS JOINT: the question asked was the wall thickness at the panel end, because a thin web would have left 2-3 mm, and 15 says the screw goes into real depth rather than through a skin. These screws only stop the bed walking, so 3 x D is far more than the duty - but it is measured, and the earlier concern was not. TT_BED_END_SCREW_L23.39 mmderived
built from TT_BED_SCREW_STACK + TT_BED_END_ENGAGE
23.43 - the M5 screw length that would use the whole tapped depth: 3.429 side plate + 5 spacer + 15 of thread. SETTLED: BUY M5 x 20. It reaches 11.57 into a hole tapped 15, which clamps with 3.4 of hole to spare. OPEN: DO NOT BUY 25 - it wants 16.57 and would bottom out 1.57 short of clamping, FEELING tight while holding nothing. FLAG: AN 18 WAS THE ANSWER UNTIL THE DEPTH WAS GAUGED: on the first figure of 10 a 20 was the part that would have bottomed, and the two recommendations are opposite. THAT IS WHY THIS SYMBOL IS NOT THE PART NUMBER - it is the ceiling, the bought screw sits under it, and the depth it depends on is measured rather than assumed.
TT_BED_END_HOLE_Z4 mmmeasured
used by TT_BED_SPACER_CLEAR
4 - the M5 hole centre in the panel end, above the torsion box's top skin. His, 2026-10-11. FLAG: IT IS WHAT MAKES THE SPACER A CLEARANCE PROBLEM: an 8 OD spacer has a 4 radius, so its bottom edge lands exactly ON the skin. See TT_BED_SPACER_CLEAR.
TT_BED_SPACER_OD8 mmpart
used by TT_BED_SPACER_CLEAR
8 - the stainless spacer's outside diameter, the one he is ordering to test. His, 2026-10-11.
TT_BED_SPACER_ID5.1 mmpart
5.1 - the spacer's bore, 0.1 over the M5 screw. His, 2026-10-11. Stainless.
TT_BED_SPACER_CLEAR0 mmderived
built from TT_BED_END_HOLE_Z - (TT_BED_SPACER_OD / 2)
FLAG: ZERO. The spacer's bottom edge is TANGENT to the top skin - 4 of hole centre against a 4 radius - and that is a coincidence rather than a fit. OPEN: IT FAILS IN THE DIRECTION THAT MATTERS: if anything goes the wrong way by a tenth the spacer rides ON the skin and LIFTS THE PANEL END OFF ITS BEDDING, which gives back the continuous support that dropping the 2040s was for. Things that can go the wrong way: the hole centre on panels he did not drill, spacer OD tolerance, glue squeeze-out or a proud veneer patch, and the skin not being flat under a 1193.8 panel. SETTLED: HE IS ORDERING SPACERS TO TEST THE FIT, 2026-10-11 - UNVERIFIED UNTIL A REAL ONE IS OFFERED UP TO A REAL PANEL. OPEN: IT CANNOT BE MARKED provenance OPEN because it has an expr, and dims.py rightly forbids that pair - the ARITHMETIC is certain and it is the CONSEQUENCE that is open, so machine/spoilboard.md carries the open item and this note carries the warning. IF IT FOULS, the fix is to RELIEVE THE SKIN and not the spacer: sixteen scallops about 10 across and 1.5 deep, one under each screw, marked off the panels at assembly. A relief in the skin cannot lift the panel; a filed spacer or a thinner one both can. A 6 OD spacer would give 1 of clearance on a 0.45 stainless wall, which these no-load screws would carry, but it depends on sourcing a part where the relief does not.
T_BB_RIB18 mmmeasured
used by SAND_PORT_STEP_END, SAND_PORT_STEP, BOX_BAY_W_END, BOX_BAY_FORE_AFT_END, RIB_LAST_FRONT
source His micrometer on the Crosscut Hardwoods sheet, 2026-10-13 - sold as 18, verified to 0.005
18 - the egg-crate ribs, cut from the THIN sheet, and they stay the thin sheet even though the thick top skin leaves surplus 24 stock. SETTLED: MEASURED 2026-10-13 - his micrometer, sold as 18, where this had been carried at a nominal 19. FLAG: ASKED AND ANSWERED 2026-10-11. The skins are the flanges and carry the bending moment; THE RIBS CARRY SHEAR, and 18 of Baltic birch is far past what the shear demands - which is the same reason torsion-box.md calls rib HEIGHT the lever and skin material the weak one. OPEN: AND THE TRADE RUNS BACKWARDS: thicker ribs DISPLACE SAND, and sand is about 1.5 g/cc against plywood's 0.68, so swapping rib volume for sand volume REDUCES total mass - paying plywood to remove ballast, on a box where mass is wanted for damping. SETTLED: THE SURPLUS 24 GOES TO THE BACK EDGE instead, which torsion-box.md names as the thing that deserves material: a beam supported at its midpoint carrying both back corners, which cannot be propped. The walls and tongues are the next best homes for it.

18 - the INNER wall lamination, the web that sits between the two skins, cut from the THIN sheet. Nothing bolts through it: the plates bolt through the OUTER lamination, which is T_BOX_TONGUE. SETTLED: MEASURED 2026-10-13 - his micrometer, sold as 18, where this had been carried at a nominal 19.

TT_BED_GROOVE_D1 mmnominal

TT_BED_GROOVE_INSET3.39 mmalias
another name for T_SIDE_PLATE
3.429 - the groove's OUTBOARD edge, inset from the box's top-skin edge. SETTLED: THE SIDE PLATE STANDS FLUSH WITH THAT EDGE, which is what makes a router fence off the box edge sufficient - no need to clamp the plate as a fence. His, 2026-10-11, and it matches the file: BOX_W is measured INSIDE the side tongues and the tongue's inside face is the plate's OUTER face, so there is no channel and the plate's outer face IS the skin's edge. FLAG: IT IS T_SIDE_PLATE AND NOT 1/8in. He said 1/8, and the plate was ORDERED 10 GAUGE at 3.429 - the entry for T_SIDE_PLATE records that a nominal 1/8 was carried there and was wrong. The 0.254 matters only because of WHICH WAY IT ERRS: insetting 3.175 starts the groove 0.254 UNDER the plate's footprint, undercutting the one edge PLATE_H calls the edge that bears on the top skin. Inset 3.5 and let the error fall inboard, where it costs nothing. OPEN: CALIPER THE DELIVERED SHEET - T_SIDE_PLATE's own note says 10 gauge is a gauge and the gauge is the nominal; the groove follows the plate in hand, not this.
TT_BED_GROOVE_W12 mmnominal
12 - the groove's width, measured inboard from TT_BED_GROOVE_INSET. The spacer is TT_BED_SPACER_OD across and TT_BED_SPACER_PART long, so anything from about 8 to 15 works and WIDER IS FREE: the panel is 250 deep and bears across all of it, so a few millimetres of relief under its outer edge is nothing. 12 is a round number inside that band rather than a fit. OPEN: THE WIDTH IS SLACK AND THE INSET IS NOT - error inboard costs nothing, error outboard undercuts the side plate's bearing edge.
POST_W_X72 mmpart
used by LEG_STRIP, BAY_OPEN_DEPTH, RAIL_CROSS_INNER_L, RAIL_CROSS_INNER_SPAN, TONGUE_BOLT_GRIP, POST_SPAN_CORNERS, TENON_SPACER_GAP, MID_TENON_BLOCK_X1, MID_TENON_BLOCK_X2, MID_TENON_BLOCK_T_MAX
source his, 2026-10-15
72 - the leg ACROSS THE MACHINE, which is the LAMINATION direction: four 18 mm Baltic birch strips face to face. SETTLED: HIS, 2026-10-15, and the four strips are the reason the figure is 72 rather than anything rounder. SETTLED: THE LAMINATION RUNS ACROSS THE MACHINE BECAUSE OF THE CROSS TENON - the base's fore-aft rails pass THROUGH the leg as a through tenon formed by omitting the middle two strips at glue-up, so the mortise is 36 wide in X and runs the full POST_W_Y in Y. A rail running front to back needs the strips stacked across the machine; the other orientation would have to have the mortise chopped. FLAG: 18 + 36 + 18 = 72 EXACTLY, so the rail is two laminations thick and the gap is two strips wide - the arithmetic is why the leg is four strips and not five. NOTE: THE MORTISES MUST STAY WELL CLEAR OF THE TOP, where the ledge and tenon are cut and the geometry assumes solid material.
POST_W_Y86 mmpart
used by TENON_CORNER_Y, SHOULDER_CORNER, TENON_MID_DEPTH, BAY_OPEN_FORE_AFT, CENTRE_WALL_PAD_L, TONGUE_BOLT_A, TONGUE_BOLT_B, POST_SPAN_FORE_AFT
86 - the leg FORE AND AFT, which is the strip's own width: ripped at 90 and trimmed to 86 once the lamination is dry. SETTLED: HIS, 2026-10-15. It is the one dimension of the old square 86 cedar post that survived, and it survived because the trim is a choice rather than a consequence - the strips are ripped oversize on purpose and brought to size after glue-up, which is also what makes the faces square. NOTE: THE OLD POST WAS 86 SQUARE and the leg is 72 x 86, so the two axes have their own symbols now. A single POST_W is retired; anything reading it was reading one axis and meaning the other.
POST_W86 mmpartretired
FLAG: RETIRED 2026-10-15 AND NOTHING DERIVES FROM IT. It was the dressed cedar 4x4's section, square at 86, and the laminated plywood leg that replaced it is 72 x 86 - see POST_W_X and POST_W_Y. Kept rather than deleted because eight symbols hung off it and the split is exactly the kind of change that leaves one of them reading the wrong axis: the corner tenon went from 44 x 44 to 30 x 44, the back tenon blocks moved, and two of the three POST_SPAN figures changed while the third did not. Historic note follows, from when the posts were cedar. SETTLED: IT IS MACHINED TO THIS, NOT MEASURED AT IT - his call 2026-10-11: he dresses all three on the Hammer A31, so 86 is a TARGET HE PRODUCES rather than a figure inherited from whatever the stick gave. FLAG: THAT RETIRES 'CUT EACH HOLE TO ITS OWN POST', which this file and assembly.md both carried: with three posts at one size the three tenon holes are identical and come off one setup, and TENON_CORNER's 42 stops being provisional. SETTLED: THE SQUARE FACES MATTER MORE THAN THE WIDTH: the ledge is a cross-cut shoulder and the perimeter wall lands directly over it, so a face out of square puts the bearing on an edge instead of a face - which is the half of the jointer's job that the width figure does not capture. OPEN: 86 WAS CHOSEN AGAIN RATHER THAN INHERITED: it is what everything already derives from, and reaching it from a nominal 88.9 S4S stick is about 1.45 off each of two faces. NOTE: TENON_HOLE_CLEAR STILL STANDS at 1 a side - machining the posts makes the holes identical, not the assembly easier; a 565 lb box still has to be lowered onto three tenons. NOTE: WHAT THIS REPLACED, 2026-10-11: the note read NOMINAL AND KNOWN TO BE OPTIMISTIC, with a standing instruction to measure the three you have and cut each hole to its own post, because a stick measuring 88.9 would give a tenon 2.9 larger than derived. Machining the posts removes that whole branch.
T_BOX_LEDGE42 mmalias
another name for T_BOX_WALL
used by TENON_CORNER_Y, SHOULDER_CORNER, SHOULDER_MID, TENON_MID_DEPTH, TENON_SPACER_GAP
44 - the ledge depth at every post, on every ledged face. FLAG: IT IS THE WALL THICKNESS BY RULE, NOT BY COINCIDENCE - torsion-box.md: 'each ledge must be as deep as the wall standing on it'. The ledges are cut on the OUTWARD-FACING faces so the perimeter wall lands directly over the shoulder and the load runs wall, skin, shoulder, post in a straight line with no plate bending. A ledge shallower than the wall would put the tenon under the wall, leaving the wall a hole where it needs to bear. SETTLED: 44 SINCE 2026-10-11, up from 38, and it moved because T_BOX_TONGUE went 19 to 25 - THIS IS THE CONSEQUENCE THAT WAS MISSED when that change was called 'geometrically free'. It is free inside the machine and not at the posts. SETTLED: EXPRESSED RATHER THAN STATED so it can never go stale against the wall again: it was 38 in three files while the wall was 44.
LEG_STRIP18 mmderived

18 - one outer strip of a leg, which is what is left either side of the slot. SETTLED: THE SLOT RUNS THE LEG'S FULL HEIGHT - his call 2026-10-15, and his reason is the good one: stopping it at the upper grid would leave a 10 mm bridge of laminated plywood spanning the slot and pretending to be the surface the box bears on. FLAG: SO THE SLOT PASSES THROUGH THE SHOULDER AND THE TENON TOO, and this symbol is what the tenon tabs and the bearing areas are now built from - not POST_W_X, which is the leg's overall width and no longer a bearing dimension anywhere.
TENON_CORNER_X18 mmalias
another name for LEG_STRIP

TENON_CORNER_Y44 mmderived

SHOULDER_CORNER1980 mm2derived

T_BACK_WALL18 mmpinned
used by SHOULDER_MID, CENTRE_WALL_NOMINAL_L
18 - the BACK shear wall, one sheet, where the central wall is two. SETTLED: 18 IS ENOUGH AND THE REASON IS THE FASTENING - a shear panel is governed by what holds its edges, not by its thickness, which is why this file's standing instruction about shear walls is glue plus screws at about 150 all the way round. Doubling the panel without doubling the fastening buys almost nothing. SETTLED: THE CENTRAL WALL IS 36 FOR A GEOMETRIC REASON THAT DOES NOT APPLY HERE - it has to FILL the leg slot. CENTRE_WALL_SCREW_COUNT18 mmpart
18 - the screws holding the central wall's two laminations, AS BUILT: 14 round the perimeter and 4 up the middle, his. GLUED AND SCREWED, so they stay in - they are not just the glue-up's clamps. CENTRE_WALL_SCREW_L31.75 mmpart
31.75 - 1.25in, the screws that hold the central wall's two laminations together. His. They do the job TWICE: first as a dry fix so the mis-cut pair can be trimmed as one, then out and back into their own holes as the clamps for the glue-up, which is what puts the panels back into the alignment the trim gave them. FLAG: THE TOP PLY WANTS A CLEARANCE HOLE so the screw pulls the plies together - biting in both it can jack them apart, and that is invisible once there is glue in the joint. It leaves about 14 of bite in the second ply and stops 4.25 short of the far face, so nothing breaks through.
CENTRE_WALL_NOMINAL_H758 mmderived
built from WALL_H - 6
758 - what the central wall's height was DRAWN at, and what the sheet reasoning was done on: at this height both laminations come off ONE 5 x 5, where at the full WALL_H they take two. FLAG: THE BINDING FIGURE WAS 759.6 - a 5 x 5 trims to 1520.8 and the 1.6 kerf between the two pieces leaves 759.6 each, so 4 under would have missed by 0.4. The 6 clears it by 1.6, a margin rather than a fit. NOTE: THE PART IS CENTRE_WALL_H AND IT IS SMALLER - kept because the drawings and the nest were worked out on this one, so a reader meeting 758 somewhere has a symbol to find it under.

BASE_BOLT_D7.94 mmpart
7.94 - 5/16in, the bolt through every dry joint in the base: both fore-aft rail tongues where they pass through a leg, and the central shear wall in the back centre leg's slot. His call, over a 1/4in dowel through the same joints - a dowel is glued in once, where a bolt with a washer and a lock nut can be snugged again after the first season's movement. FLAG: THESE DO NOT GO IN fasteners.toml. That schedule renders every size as M-something by construction, so an imperial entry would print as a metric bolt that does not exist.
BASE_BOLT_WASHER0 mmOPEN
OPEN: FENDER WASHERS BOTH ENDS, DIAMETER NOT PICKED. A plain 5/16in washer dishes into plywood face at torque, and once it dishes the joint is loose again - which defeats the whole point of choosing a bolt that can be re-snugged. The clamp wanted is snug plus the lock nut, not crushed. Size it off what is on the shelf.
TONGUE_BOLT_COUNT2 mmpinned
2 - bolts through each fore-aft rail tongue where it passes through a leg's slot. SETTLED: TWO, NOT ONE - his question. ONE BOLT IS A PIN: it carries withdrawal but the rail can still rotate about it, and the only thing resisting that is the housed fit in a DRY slot that has to survive seasonal movement and the base being rolled. Two spaced along the tongue make the joint non-rotating on its own and halve the bearing load per hole, which is what matters in plywood - the limit is the hole wall crushing, not the bolt.

BOX_RIBS_FORE_AFT4 mmnominal
